{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_vrtx_01YDKhshwEySsRTtVPaPGAnr","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01GLjPdp9ZMZYYmJruTeKGPt","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/excel-economic-modeling.md","content":"# Excel Economic Modeling Guide\n\n## Overview\nThis guide provides practical techniques for implementing economic models in Excel, specifically tailored for production function analysis and macroeconomic modeling tasks.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Cobb-Douglas Production Function Implementation\n\n### Theory\nThe Cobb-Douglas production function is:\n```\nY = A * K^α * L^(1-α)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- Y = Output (Real GDP)\n- A = Total Factor Productivity (TFP)\n- K = Capital Stock\n- L = Labor Force\n- α = Capital share parameter (typically 0.3-0.4)\n- (1-α) = Labor share parameter\n\n### Excel Implementation\n\n#### Basic Formula Setup\n\n**Step 1: Define Parameters**\n- Cell A1: `Alpha` → Value in B1 (e.g., 0.35)\n- Cell A2: `TFP (A)` → Value in B2 (e.g., 1.0)\n- Cell A3: `Capital Stock (K)` → Value in B3\n- Cell A4: `Labor Force (L)` → Value in B4\n\n**Step 2: Calculate Output**\n\nIn cell B5, enter the Cobb-Douglas formula:\n```excel\n=B2 * (B3^B1) * (B4^(1-B1))\n```\n\nThis directly implements Y = A * K^α * L^(1-α)\n\n#### Logarithmic Transformation for Estimation\n\nFor estimation purposes, take natural logs:\n```\nln(Y) = ln(A) + α*ln(K) + (1-α)*ln(L)\n```\n\nExcel formulas:\n```excel\n=LN(output_cell)\n=LN(capital_cell)\n=LN(labor_cell)\n```\n\n#### Time Series Implementation\n\nCreate a table structure:\n\n| Year | K | L | ln(K) | ln(L) | ln(Y) | A | Y |\n|------|---|---|-------|-------|-------|---|---|\n| 2000 | 1000 | 100 | =LN(B2) | =LN(C2) | =LN(H2) | | =D2*(B2^0.35)*(C2^0.65) |\n| 2001 | 1050 | 102 | =LN(B3) | =LN(C3) | =LN(H3) | | =D3*(B3^0.35)*(C3^0.65) |\n\n**Key Formulas:**\n- Capital: `=B2*0.95 + Investment` (with depreciation rate ~5%)\n- TFP: `=Output / (K^0.35 * L^0.65)`\n- Growth Rate: `=(Current - Previous) / Previous * 100`\n\n### Handling Depreciation\n\nCapital stock evolves as:\n```\nK(t) = K(t-1) * (1 - δ) + I(t)\n```\n\nWhere δ = depreciation rate (typically 0.05 for 5%)\n\nExcel formula (in column B for Capital, starting at B3):\n```excel\n=B2*(1-0.05) + I2\n```\n\nWhere I2 is investment in column I.\n\n---\n\n## 2. HP Filter Implementation in Excel Using Solver\n\n### Theory\nThe Hodrick-Prescott (HP) Filter separates a time series into:\n- **Trend Component** (smooth long-term movement)\n- **Cyclical Component** (deviations from trend)\n\nThe filter minimizes:\n```\nL = Σ(Y_t - T_t)² + λ*Σ[(T_{t+1} - T_t) - (T_t - T_{t-1})]²\n```\n\nWhere:\n- Y_t = actual value\n- T_t = trend value\n- λ = smoothing parameter (1600 for quarterly data, 100 for annual)\n\n### Excel Implementation Steps\n\n#### Setup Data Table\n\nCreate columns:\n- A: Year\n- B: Original Series (Y_t)\n- C: Trend (T_t) - to be optimized\n- D: Cycle (Y_t - T_t)\n- E: Squared Cycle (D²)\n- F: Smoothness Penalty\n\n#### Initialize Trend Column\n\nStarting values can be the original series itself or a simple moving average:\n```excel\n=IF(ROW()=2, B2, B2)  // For first value\n=AVERAGE(B2:B4)       // For moving average initialization\n```\n\n#### Calculate Cycle Component\n\nIn column D (starting D2):\n```excel\n=B2-C2\n```\n\nCopy down for all periods.\n\n#### Calculate Squared Deviations\n\nIn column E (E2):\n```excel\n=D2^2\n```\n\n#### Calculate Smoothness Penalty\n\nIn column F (starting F3 for trend difference):\n```excel\n=((C3-C2)-(C2-C1))^2\n```\n\nThis penalizes second differences in the trend.\n\n#### Set Up Objective Function\n\nIn a designated cell (e.g., H2), create the loss function:\n```excel\n=SUM(E:E) + 1600*SUM(F:F)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- SUM(E:E) = sum of squared cycles\n- 1600 = λ parameter (smoothing weight)\n- SUM(F:F) = sum of smoothness penalties\n\n### Running Solver\n\n**Steps in Excel:**\n1. Go to **Data** → **Solver** (or **Tools** → **Solver**)\n2. Set Objective Cell: H2 (your loss function)\n3. Select: **Min** (minimize)\n4. By Changing Cells: C2:C[last_row] (trend column)\n5. Click **Solve**\n\n**Solver Settings:**\n- Engine: GRG Nonlinear\n- Precision: 0.0001\n- Iterations: 100 (or auto)\n- Check \"Make Unconstrained Variables Non-Negative\" if appropriate\n\n**Result:** Column C will contain the HP-filtered trend.\n\n### Extracting Results\n\nAfter solving:\n- **Trend values** = Column C\n- **Cyclical component** = Column D (=B-C)\n- **Cyclical contribution %** = `=D/B*100`\n\n### Plotting\n\nCreate two charts:\n1. **Original vs Trend**: Plot columns B and C\n2. **Cyclical Component**: Plot column D (shows deviations)\n\n---\n\n## 3. Linking Cells Across Sheets\n\n### Basic Sheet References\n\n#### Simple Cell Reference\n```excel\n=Sheet2!A1\n```\n\n#### Named Range Reference (Recommended)\n1. Select cell/range on source sheet\n2. Go to **Sheet** → **Named Ranges and Expressions** → **Define**\n3. Enter name (e.g., `GDP_2000`)\n4. In other sheet, reference by: `=GDP_2000`\n\n#### Dynamic Range Reference\n```excel\n=INDIRECT(\"Sheet2!A\"&ROW())\n```\n\nThis references the same row number in Sheet2, column A.\n\n### Multi-Sheet Calculations\n\n**Example: Aggregating Data from Multiple Sheets**\n\nIn Summary sheet, cell A1:\n```excel\n=Sheet1!B5 + Sheet2!B5 + Sheet3!B5\n```\n\nThis sums the same cell from three different sheets.\n\n### Creating Data Connections\n\n**Best Practice Structure:**\n\n```\nSheet 1: Raw Data\n  - Column A: Years\n  - Column B: Raw GDP values\n\nSheet 2: Processed Data\n  - Column A: =Sheet1!A:A (linked)\n  - Column B: =Sheet1!B:B (linked)\n  - Column C: Calculations based on linked data\n\nSheet 3: Analysis\n  - References to key cells in Sheet2\n  - Summary statistics\n```\n\n### Dynamic Named Ranges\n\nIn name manager, define:\n```excel\nRange: =OFFSET(Sheet1!$A$1, 0, 0, COUNTA(Sheet1!$A:$A), 1)\nName: Data_Range\n```\n\nThen reference: `=SUM(Data_Range)`\n\n---\n\n## 4. Creating Dynamic Formulas for Projections\n\n### FORECAST Function (Deprecated) → Use FORECAST.LINEAR\n\nFor simple linear projection:\n```excel\n=FORECAST.LINEAR(x_value, known_y_range, known_x_range)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```excel\n=FORECAST.LINEAR(2025, B2:B20, A2:A20)\n```\n\nProjects 2025 value based on historical trend 2000-2020.\n\n### TREND Function\n\nFor polynomial trend projections:\n```excel\n=TREND(known_y_values, known_x_values, new_x_values, [const])\n```\n\n**Example for Multi-Period Projection:**\n\n| Year | GDP | Projection |\n|------|-----|-----------|\n| ... | ... | |\n| 2020 | 1000 | |\n| 2021 | | =TREND($B$2:$B$20, $A$2:$A$20, A21) |\n| 2022 | | =TREND($B$2:$B$20, $A$2:$A$20, A22) |\n| 2023 | | =TREND($B$2:$B$20, $A$2:$A$20, A23) |\n\n### Compound Growth Rate Projection\n\nFor constant growth rate g:\n```excel\n=Starting_Value * (1 + growth_rate)^years_ahead\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```excel\n=B20 * (1.02)^5  // Project 5 years at 2% growth\n```\n\nTo calculate implied growth rate from historical data:\n```excel\n=POWER(Ending_Value/Starting_Value, 1/Years) - 1\n```\n\n### Scenario Projection Model\n\nSet up parameters in a separate section:\n\n| Parameter | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Base GDP 2020 | 1000 |\n| Growth Rate (%) | 2.5 |\n| Years to Project | 5 |\n\nProjection table:\n```excel\nYear: =2020 + ROW() - 2\nGDP: =IF(ROW()=2, $B$1, OFFSET(INDIRECT(\"A\"&ROW()-1), 0, 1) * (1 + $B$2))\n```\n\n### Multi-Variable Projections with Cobb-Douglas\n\nProject output using projections of inputs:\n\n| Year | K_proj | L_proj | A_proj | Y_proj |\n|------|--------|--------|--------|--------|\n| 2021 | [formula] | [formula] | [formula] | =D2*(B2^0.35)*(C2^0.65) |\n\nWhere each projection column uses TREND or growth rates.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Using TREND Function for Trend Extensions\n\n### Basic TREND Syntax\n\n```excel\n=TREND(y_values, x_values, new_x_values, [constant])\n```\n\nParameters:\n- **y_values**: Dependent variable (e.g., GDP)\n- **x_values**: Independent variable (e.g., years)\n- **new_x_values**: Points where to calculate trend\n- **[constant]**: TRUE (default) for y-intercept, FALSE for through origin\n\n### Single Value Projection\n\nProject 2025 based on 2000-2020 data:\n```excel\n=TREND(B2:B21, A2:A21, 2025)\n```\n\n### Array Projection (Multiple Years)\n\nTo project 5 years forward:\n\n1. Select cells where results will go (e.g., B22:B26)\n2. Enter array formula:\n```excel\n=TREND(B2:B21, A2:A21, A22:A26)\n```\n3. Press **Ctrl+Shift+Enter** (creates array formula)\n\n### Comparison: TREND vs FORECAST.LINEAR\n\n| Feature | TREND | FORECAST.LINEAR |\n|---------|-------|-----------------|\n| Linear fit | Yes | Yes |\n| Array output | Yes | No (single value) |\n| Through origin | Optional | No |\n| Multiple periods | Efficient | Loop required |\n| Clarity | Good | Better naming |\n\n### Practical Example: Multi-Period GDP Projection\n\n**Historical Data (2000-2020):**\n- A2:A21 = Years\n- B2:B21 = GDP values\n\n**Setup Projection:**\n\nYears to project (A22:A26):\n```excel\n2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025\n```\n\nTrend projection (B22:B26) - enter as array formula:\n```excel\n{=TREND(B$2:B$21, A$2:A$21, A22:A26)}\n```\n\nCycle component would be:\n```excel\n=B22 - C22  // Actual vs Trend\n```\n\n### Polynomial TREND Extensions\n\nFor higher-order trends (though TREND uses linear regression):\n- Use multiple X columns with polynomial powers\n- Or use LOGEST for exponential trends\n\n**Exponential Trend with LOGEST:**\n```excel\n=LOGEST(B2:B21, A2:A21)\n```\n\nReturns growth coefficients for exponential fit.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Panel Data Organization\n\n### Data Structure\n\nBest practice for panel data (multiple entities, multiple time periods):\n\n**Long Format (Recommended for Excel Analysis):**\n\n| Entity | Year | Variable1 | Variable2 | Variable3 |\n|--------|------|-----------|-----------|-----------|\n| USA | 2000 | 10000 | 1500 | 0.35 |\n| USA | 2001 | 10300 | 1520 | 0.35 |\n| UK | 2000 | 2500 | 400 | 0.36 |\n| UK | 2001 | 2600 | 410 | 0.36 |\n\n**Wide Format (For Some Analyses):**\n\n| Year | USA_GDP | USA_K | UK_GDP | UK_K |\n|------|---------|-------|--------|-------|\n| 2000 | 10000 | 1500 | 2500 | 400 |\n| 2001 | 10300 | 1520 | 2600 | 410 |\n\n### Sorting and Filtering Panel Data\n\n1. **Sort by Entity, then Year:**\n   - Select all data including headers\n   - **Data** → **Sort**\n   - Sort Key 1: Entity (A→Z)\n   - Sort Key 2: Year (smallest to largest)\n\n2. **Create Entity-Year Index (helpful for lookups):**\n   ```excel\n   =Entity_Code & \"-\" & Year\n   ```\n\n### Panel Data Calculations\n\n#### Calculate Changes Within Entity\n\nFor growth rates within each country:\n```excel\n=IF(A2=A1, (B2-B1)/B1, \"\")\n```\n\nThis calculates growth only if same entity (A=A).\n\n#### Entity-Specific Means\n\nUsing AVERAGEIFS:\n```excel\n=AVERAGEIFS(Variable_Column, Entity_Column, \"USA\", Year_Column, \">=2010\")\n```\n\nCalculates average of Variable for USA, 2010 onward.\n\n#### Fixed Effects (Demeaning)\n\nCreate entity-specific mean column:\n```excel\n=AVERAGEIF($A$2:$A$100, A2, B$2:$B$100)\n```\n\nDemeaned variable (for fixed effects):\n```excel\n=B2 - [Entity Mean]\n```\n\n### Panel Data Summary Statistics\n\nCreate summary table by entity:\n\n| Entity | Count | Mean | Min | Max | Std Dev |\n|--------|-------|------|-----|-----|---------|\n| USA | =COUNTIF() | =AVERAGEIF() | =MIN(IF()) | =MAX(IF()) | |\n\nFormulas example:\n```excel\nCount: =COUNTIF($A$2:$A$100, \"USA\")\nMean: =AVERAGEIF($A$2:$A$100, \"USA\", $B$2:$B$100)\nMin: =MINIFS($B$2:$B$100, $A$2:$A$100, \"USA\")\nMax: =MAXIFS($B$2:$B$100, $A$2:$A$100, \"USA\")\n```\n\n### Converting Between Long and Wide Format\n\n#### Long to Wide (Pivot Table Method)\n1. Select data range\n2. **Insert** → **Pivot Table**\n3. Rows: Entity\n4. Columns: Year\n5. Values: Variable (sum or average)\n\n#### Long to Wide (Formula Method)\n\nIn wide format sheet:\n```excel\n=INDEX(LongData, MATCH(1, (Entity=Entity_Header) * (Year=Year_Header), 0), Variable_Column)\n```\n\nEnter as array formula (Ctrl+Shift+Enter).\n\n### Working with Multiple Countries\n\n**Separate Worksheets Approach:**\n- Sheet \"USA\": All USA data\n- Sheet \"UK\": All UK data\n- Sheet \"Summary\": References to key cells from each country sheet\n\n**Consolidated Approach (Recommended):**\n- Single worksheet with long format\n- Use filter dropdowns or slicers to view specific countries\n- Create pivot tables for summaries\n\n### Panel Regression in Excel\n\nFor panel analysis with fixed effects:\n\n1. Create demeaned variables (subtract entity means)\n2. Use linear regression (Data Analysis ToolPak) on demeaned data\n3. Or use LINEST function:\n\n```excel\n=LINEST(demeaned_Y, demeaned_X_range, FALSE, TRUE)\n```\n\nReturns regression coefficients accounting for fixed effects.\n\n---\n\n## Quick Reference: Formula Cheat Sheet\n\n| Task | Formula |\n|------|---------|\n| Cobb-Douglas Y | `=A * (K^α) * (L^(1-α))` |\n| Capital with depreciation | `=K_prev * (1 - δ) + I` |\n| Growth rate | `=(New - Old) / Old * 100` |\n| HP Filter Loss | `=SUM(cycles^2) + λ*SUM(trends_2nd_diff^2)` |\n| Simple projection | `=TREND(Y_historical, X_historical, X_new)` |\n| Cross-sheet reference | `=Sheet2!A1` |\n| Conditional average | `=AVERAGEIF(range, criteria, average_range)` |\n| Demeaning | `=Value - AVERAGEIF(Entity_column, current_entity, Variable)` |\n\n---\n\n## Tips and Best Practices\n\n1. **Always use absolute references ($) for parameters** to avoid accidental changes when copying\n2. **Create separate parameter sheets** for easy scenario analysis\n3. **Use named ranges** for complex models for better readability\n4. **Validate inputs** with data validation rules\n5. **Document all assumptions** in comments or separate documentation cells\n6. **Version control**: Save scenarios with different names (e.g., \"Model_v1.xlsx\", \"Model_Scenario_HighGrowth.xlsx\")\n7. **Test with simple examples first** before implementing on full datasets\n8. **Use conditional formatting** to highlight anomalies or out-of-range values\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":6,"cache_creation_input_tokens":120,"cache_read_input_tokens":11654,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":120,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01JFooZaZBzWCj51SUgqTUGM","session_id":"c3e9eb62-96a7-4e81-a399-d9247689a4b2","uuid":"e451d0b1-0897-490c-a57e-b5740133aad7"}

It covers programmatic access methods, data parsing, and validation.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Accessing IMF WEO Database\n\n### Overview\nThe IMF World Economic Outlook (WEO) database contains macroeconomic data for 190+ countries including:\n- Real GDP growth\n- Current account balance\n- Employment rates\n- Inflation rates\n- Public debt\n- Exchange rates\n\n### IMF WEO Portal Access\n\n#### Web Interface (Manual)\n1. Visit: https://www.imf.org/external/datamapper/api/v1\n2. Search for specific indicators and countries\n3. Download as Excel or CSV\n\n**Available Indicators:**\n- `NGDP` - Nominal GDP\n- `NGDP_D` - Nominal GDP (domestic currency)\n- `NGDPD` - Nominal GDP (current prices, USD)\n- `NGDP_RPCH` - Real GDP growth\n- `PCPIPCH` - Inflation rate (percent change)\n- `LP` - Labor force\n- `PCPIE` - Employment rate\n- `GGX_NGDP` - General government expenditure (% of GDP)\n\n#### Programmatic Access (Python)\n\n**Using requests library:**\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport json\nimport pandas as pd\n\n# IMF API endpoint\nurl = \"https://www.imf.org/external/datamapper/api/v1/NGDPD\"\n\n# Request all countries\nresponse = requests.get(url)\ndata = response.json()\n\n# Extract data for specific country (USA)\nusa_data = data['values']['NGDPD']['USA']\n\n# Convert to DataFrame\ndf = pd.DataFrame.from_dict(usa_data, orient='index', columns=['Value'])\ndf.index.name = 'Year'\nprint(df)\n```\n\n**Accessing Multiple Indicators:**\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport pandas as pd\n\ndef get_imf_data(indicators, country_codes):\n    \"\"\"\n    Fetch IMF WEO data for multiple indicators and countries\n\n    Parameters:\n    - indicators: list of indicator codes (e.g., ['NGDPD', 'PCPIPCH'])\n    - country_codes: list of ISO country codes (e.g., ['USA', 'GBR', 'DEU'])\n    \"\"\"\n    base_url = \"https://www.imf.org/external/datamapper/api/v1\"\n\n    all_data = {}\n\n    for indicator in indicators:\n        url = f\"{base_url}/{indicator}\"\n        response = requests.get(url)\n        data = response.json()\n\n        # Extract data for specific countries\n        indicator_data = {}\n        for country in country_codes:\n            if country in data['values'][indicator]:\n                indicator_data[country] = data['values'][indicator][country]\n\n        all_data[indicator] = indicator_data\n\n    return all_data\n\n# Example usage\nindicators = ['NGDPD', 'NGDP_RPCH', 'PCPIPCH']\ncountries = ['USA', 'GBR', 'DEU', 'FRA', 'JPN']\n\nimf_data = get_imf_data(indicators, countries)\n\n# Create panel DataFrame\ncombined_data = []\nfor indicator, countries_data in imf_data.items():\n    for country, yearly_values in countries_data.items():\n        for year, value in yearly_values.items():\n            combined_data.append({\n                'Year': int(year),\n                'Country': country,\n                'Indicator': indicator,\n                'Value': float(value)\n            })\n\ndf = pd.DataFrame(combined_data)\nprint(df.head(20))\n```\n\n### IMF Data Validation Checks\n\nAfter downloading:\n\n```python\ndef validate_imf_data(df):\n    \"\"\"Basic validation for IMF data\"\"\"\n\n    # Check for NaN values\n    missing = df.isnull().sum()\n    print(f\"Missing values:\\n{missing}\")\n\n    # Check year range\n    print(f\"Year range: {df['Year'].min()} to {df['Year'].max()}\")\n\n    # Check country count\n    print(f\"Countries: {df['Country'].nunique()}\")\n\n    # Outlier detection (values > 5 std deviations)\n    for indicator in df['Indicator'].unique():\n        subset = df[df['Indicator'] == indicator]['Value']\n        mean = subset.mean()\n        std = subset.std()\n        outliers = subset[(subset > mean + 5*std) | (subset < mean - 5*std)]\n        if len(outliers) > 0:\n            print(f\"Outliers in {indicator}: {len(outliers)} values\")\n\n    return True\n\nvalidate_imf_data(df)\n```\n\n### Caching IMF Data\n\nTo avoid repeated downloads:\n\n```python\nimport pickle\nimport os\nfrom datetime import datetime\n\ndef cache_imf_data(indicators, countries, cache_dir='./imf_cache'):\n    \"\"\"Cache IMF data with timestamp\"\"\"\n\n    cache_file = os.path.join(cache_dir, 'imf_data.pkl')\n\n    if os.path.exists(cache_file):\n        # Check age - if older than 30 days, refresh\n        file_age = datetime.now() - datetime.fromtimestamp(os.path.getctime(cache_file))\n        if file_age.days < 30:\n            with open(cache_file, 'rb') as f:\n                return pickle.load(f)\n\n    # Download fresh data\n    data = get_imf_data(indicators, countries)\n\n    # Save to cache\n    os.makedirs(cache_dir, exist_ok=True)\n    with open(cache_file, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f)\n\n    return data\n```\n\n---\n\n## 2. Parsing ECB Data Sources\n\n### Overview\nThe European Central Bank (ECB) provides economic and financial data including:\n- Exchange rates\n- Interest rates\n- Money supply\n- Employment statistics\n- Consumer price indices\n\n### ECB Statistical Data Warehouse (SDW)\n\n#### Web Interface\n1. Visit: https://sdw.ecb.europa.eu/\n2. Select dataset (e.g., \"Exchange rates\")\n3. Filter by currency, frequency (daily/monthly/quarterly)\n4. Download as CSV or Excel\n\n**Common ECB Datasets:**\n- `EXR` - Exchange rates\n- `ICP` - HICP (Harmonized Index of Consumer Prices)\n- `LFS` - Labour Force Survey\n- `MIR` - Money Market Interest Rates\n- `RT` - Reference rates\n\n### ECB API Access\n\n#### SDMX-JSON API\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport pandas as pd\n\ndef get_ecb_exchange_rate(from_currency='USD', to_currency='EUR', start_date='2000', end_date='2023'):\n    \"\"\"\n    Fetch ECB exchange rate data via SDMX API\n    \"\"\"\n\n    url = f\"https://www.ecb.europa.eu/data/eurofxref/eurofxref.zip\"\n\n    # Download daily rates\n    import zipfile\n    from io import BytesIO\n\n    response = requests.get(url)\n    zip_file = zipfile.ZipFile(BytesIO(response.content))\n\n    # Read XML file\n    with zip_file.open('eurofxref.xml') as f:\n        import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET\n        tree = ET.parse(f)\n        root = tree.getroot()\n\n        # Parse exchange rates\n        rates_data = []\n        for cube in root.findall('.//{http://www.ecb.int/vocabulary/2002-08-01/eurofxref}Cube'):\n            attrs = cube.attrib\n            if 'time' in attrs and 'currency' in attrs:\n                rates_data.append({\n                    'Date': attrs['time'],\n                    'Currency': attrs['currency'],\n                    'Rate': float(attrs['rate'])\n                })\n\n        df = pd.DataFrame(rates_data)\n        return df\n\n# Example usage\nexchange_rates = get_ecb_exchange_rate()\nprint(exchange_rates[exchange_rates['Currency'] == 'USD'].head())\n```\n\n#### SDMX-ML Query Format\n\nFor direct REST API queries:\n\n```python\nimport requests\n\ndef query_ecb_sdmx(dataset_code, dimensions):\n    \"\"\"\n    Query ECB SDMX API\n\n    Parameters:\n    - dataset_code: e.g., 'EXR' for exchange rates\n    - dimensions: dict with dimension filters\n    \"\"\"\n\n    base_url = \"https://data-api.ecb.europa.eu/service/data\"\n\n    # Build dimension string\n    dim_string = \"+\".join([f\"{k}.{v}\" for k, v in dimensions.items()])\n\n    url = f\"{base_url}/{dataset_code}/{dim_string}\"\n\n    # Add parameters\n    params = {\n        'format': 'jsondata',\n        'detail': 'full',\n        'detail': 'full'\n    }\n\n    headers = {'Accept': 'application/json'}\n\n    response = requests.get(url, params=params, headers=headers)\n    data = response.json()\n\n    return data\n\n# Example: Get EUR/USD exchange rate\ndimensions = {\n    'M.USD.EUR.SP00.A': None  # Monthly, USD, EUR, spot rate, standardized\n}\n\n# Alternative simpler format\nurl = \"https://data-api.ecb.europa.eu/service/data/EXR/M.USD.EUR.SP00.A\"\nresponse = requests.get(url, headers={'Accept': 'application/json'})\ndata = response.json()\n```\n\n#### Parsing ECB SDMX-JSON Response\n\n```python\ndef parse_ecb_sdmx_json(response_json):\n    \"\"\"Convert ECB SDMX-JSON to pandas DataFrame\"\"\"\n\n    import pandas as pd\n\n    data = response_json['data']['dataSets'][0]\n    structure = response_json['structure']\n\n    # Extract observations\n    observations = []\n    for key, obs in data['observations'].items():\n        dimensions = key.split(':')\n        for idx, value in enumerate(obs):\n            observations.append({\n                'Time': dimensions[0],  # Usually first dimension is time\n                'Value': value[0] if value else None\n            })\n\n    df = pd.DataFrame(observations)\n    df['Time'] = pd.to_datetime(df['Time'], format='%Y-%m')\n    return df.sort_values('Time')\n\n# Usage\nresponse = requests.get(url, headers={'Accept': 'application/json'})\ndata = response.json()\ndf = parse_ecb_sdmx_json(data)\nprint(df.head())\n```\n\n### ECB Data Cleaning\n\n```python\ndef clean_ecb_data(df):\n    \"\"\"Clean and validate ECB data\"\"\"\n\n    # Remove NaN values\n    df = df.dropna(subset=['Value'])\n\n    # Convert to numeric (handle any string values)\n    df['Value'] = pd.to_numeric(df['Value'], errors='coerce')\n\n    # Remove extreme outliers (likely data errors)\n    Q1 = df['Value'].quantile(0.25)\n    Q3 = df['Value'].quantile(0.75)\n    IQR = Q3 - Q1\n    df = df[(df['Value'] >= Q1 - 3*IQR) & (df['Value'] <= Q3 + 3*IQR)]\n\n    return df\n\ndf_clean = clean_ecb_data(df)\n```\n\n---\n\n## 3. Extracting PWT (Penn World Table) Data\n\n### Overview\nPenn World Table is a comprehensive database of productivity and economic data covering 183 countries from 1950 onwards. Key variables:\n- Real GDP per capita\n- Capital stock\n- Employment (number of workers)\n- Human capital index\n- Trade openness\n- TFP (Total Factor Productivity)\n\n### PWT Website Access\n\n#### Direct Download\n1. Visit: https://www.rug.nl/ggdc/productivity/pwt/\n2. Download latest version (currently 10.0)\n3. Available in Excel and CSV formats\n\n#### Data Dictionary\n- `cgdpe` - Real consumption of goods and services (chain, 2017 USD)\n- `cgdpo` - Output-side real GDP at chained PPPs (2017 USD)\n- `cn` - Capital stock at constant PPPs (2017 USD)\n- `emp` - Number of workers\n- `avh` - Average hours worked by persons engaged\n- `ctfp` - Consumption-side TFP\n- `rtfpna` - TFP at constant national prices\n\n### Programmatic Access\n\n```python\nimport pandas as pd\nimport os\n\ndef load_pwt_data(filepath='pwt10.0.xlsx', sheet_name='Data'):\n    \"\"\"Load Penn World Table data\"\"\"\n\n    df = pd.read_excel(filepath, sheet_name=sheet_name)\n\n    # Ensure correct column names\n    print(f\"Columns: {df.columns.tolist()}\")\n\n    # Display first rows\n    print(df.head())\n\n    return df\n\n# Load PWT\npwt_data = load_pwt_data('path/to/pwt10.0.xlsx')\n\n# Filter for specific countries\ncountries_of_interest = ['USA', 'GBR', 'DEU', 'FRA', 'JPN', 'IND', 'CHN']\npwt_filtered = pwt_data[pwt_data['countrycode'].isin(countries_of_interest)]\n\n# Filter for recent years\npwt_recent = pwt_filtered[pwt_filtered['year'] >= 2000]\n\nprint(pwt_recent.head(20))\n```\n\n### Calculating Key Variables from PWT\n\n#### Real GDP Growth Rate\n\n```python\ndef calculate_gdp_growth(df):\n    \"\"\"Calculate real GDP growth rate\"\"\"\n\n    df = df.sort_values(['countrycode', 'year'])\n\n    # Calculate log-difference (more stable than percent change)\n    df['ln_rgdpe'] = np.log(df['cgdpe'])\n    df['gdp_growth'] = df.groupby('countrycode')['ln_rgdpe'].diff() * 100\n\n    return df\n\npwt_with_growth = calculate_gdp_growth(pwt_recent)\nprint(pwt_with_growth[['countrycode', 'year', 'cgdpe', 'gdp_growth']].head(20))\n```\n\n#### Capital Productivity\n\n```python\ndef calculate_capital_productivity(df):\n    \"\"\"Calculate output per unit of capital\"\"\"\n\n    # Avoid division by zero\n    df['capital_productivity'] = df['cgdpe'] / df['cn']\n\n    # Log form for analysis\n    df['ln_capital_prod'] = np.log(df['capital_productivity'])\n\n    return df\n\npwt_productivity = calculate_capital_productivity(pwt_recent)\n```\n\n#### Capital-Labor Ratio and Intensity\n\n```python\ndef calculate_capital_labor_ratio(df):\n    \"\"\"Calculate capital stock per worker\"\"\"\n\n    df['capital_per_worker'] = df['cn'] / df['emp']\n    df['ln_capital_per_worker'] = np.log(df['capital_per_worker'])\n\n    return df\n\npwt_capital_labor = calculate_capital_labor_ratio(pwt_recent)\n```\n\n#### Working with Missing Data in PWT\n\n```python\ndef handle_pwt_missing_data(df, min_years=5):\n    \"\"\"Handle missing values in PWT\"\"\"\n\n    # Count non-null values per country\n    country_coverage = df.groupby('countrycode').size()\n\n    # Keep only countries with minimum data coverage\n    countries_to_keep = country_coverage[country_coverage >= min_years].index\n    df_filtered = df[df['countrycode'].isin(countries_to_keep)]\n\n    # For analysis, fill missing values using interpolation\n    df_filled = df_filtered.copy()\n    for col in ['cgdpe', 'cn', 'emp']:\n        df_filled[col] = df_filled.groupby('countrycode')[col].interpolate(\n            method='linear',\n            limit_direction='both'\n        )\n\n    return df_filled\n\npwt_complete = handle_pwt_missing_data(pwt_recent)\n```\n\n### PWT Data Validation\n\n```python\ndef validate_pwt_data(df):\n    \"\"\"Validate Penn World Table data\"\"\"\n\n    # Check for negative values (which shouldn't exist)\n    negative_cols = ['cgdpe', 'cn', 'emp', 'avh']\n    for col in negative_cols:\n        if (df[col] < 0).any():\n            print(f\"Warning: Negative values found in {col}\")\n\n    # Check extreme values\n    for col in ['cgdpe', 'cn', 'emp']:\n        mean = df[col].mean()\n        std = df[col].std()\n        outliers = df[(df[col] > mean + 5*std) | (df[col] < mean - 5*std)]\n        if len(outliers) > 0:\n            print(f\"Outliers in {col}: {len(outliers)} observations\")\n\n    # Check data completeness\n    for country in df['countrycode'].unique():\n        country_data = df[df['countrycode'] == country]\n        print(f\"{country}: {len(country_data)} observations from {country_data['year'].min()} to {country_data['year'].max()}\")\n\n    return True\n\nvalidate_pwt_data(pwt_complete)\n```\n\n---\n\n## 4. Handling International Economic Databases\n\n### General Principles for Economic Databases\n\n#### Data Harmonization\n\nDifferent databases may report the same variables differently (USD, national currency, PPP, etc.):\n\n```python\ndef harmonize_currency(df, from_currency='LCU', to_currency='USD_PPP',\n                       exchange_rates=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Convert between currency representations\n\n    Parameters:\n    - from_currency: 'LCU' (local currency), 'USD', 'USD_PPP'\n    - to_currency: target currency\n    - exchange_rates: dict with conversion rates\n    \"\"\"\n\n    if from_currency == 'LCU' and to_currency == 'USD':\n        if exchange_rates is None:\n            raise ValueError(\"Exchange rates required\")\n\n        df_converted = df.copy()\n        for country in df['countrycode'].unique():\n            if country in exchange_rates:\n                mask = df_converted['countrycode'] == country\n                df_converted.loc[mask, 'value'] *= exchange_rates[country]\n\n        return df_converted\n\n    return df\n\n# Usage with external exchange rates\nexchange_rates = {'DEU': 1.1, 'FRA': 1.15}  # EUR to USD rates\ndf_usd = harmonize_currency(df_lcm, 'LCU', 'USD', exchange_rates)\n```\n\n#### Time Alignment\n\nDifferent databases have different frequency (annual, quarterly, monthly):\n\n```python\nimport pandas as pd\n\ndef align_to_annual(df, frequency='Q'):\n    \"\"\"Convert quarterly or monthly data to annual\"\"\"\n\n    if frequency == 'Q':\n        # Average quarterly data to annual\n        df['year'] = df.index.year\n        df_annual = df.groupby(['countrycode', 'year']).agg({\n            'value': 'mean'\n        }).reset_index()\n\n    elif frequency == 'M':\n        # Average monthly data to annual\n        df['year'] = df.index.year\n        df_annual = df.groupby(['countrycode', 'year']).agg({\n            'value': 'mean'\n        }).reset_index()\n\n    return df_annual\n\n# For index data (not average)\ndef year_end_value(df, frequency='Q'):\n    \"\"\"Get year-end values (for stock variables)\"\"\"\n\n    df['year'] = df.index.year\n    df['quarter'] = df.index.quarter if frequency == 'Q' else 12\n\n    # Get December (Q4) values\n    df_annual = df[df['quarter'] == (4 if frequency == 'Q' else 12)].copy()\n\n    return df_annual.reset_index(drop=True)\n```\n\n#### Multi-Source Merging\n\n```python\ndef merge_multiple_sources(source1, source2, source3,\n                          key_cols=['countrycode', 'year']):\n    \"\"\"Merge multiple economic databases\"\"\"\n\n    # Start with source1\n    merged = source1.copy()\n\n    # Merge source2\n    merged = merged.merge(source2, on=key_cols, how='outer')\n\n    # Merge source3\n    merged = merged.merge(source3, on=key_cols, how='outer')\n\n    # For conflicting variables, prioritize sources in order\n    # Handle duplicate columns from merge\n    for col in merged.columns:\n        if col.endswith('_x'):\n            base_col = col[:-2]\n            if f'{base_col}_y' in merged.columns:\n                # Fill nulls in _x with _y values\n                merged[base_col] = merged[f'{base_col}_x'].fillna(merged[f'{base_col}_y'])\n                merged = merged.drop([f'{base_col}_x', f'{base_col}_y'], axis=1)\n\n    return merged\n\n# Merge IMF, ECB, and PWT data\ncombined = merge_multiple_sources(imf_data, ecb_data, pwt_data)\n```\n\n### Database-Specific Issues\n\n#### IMF WEO Issues\n- **Forecast data**: WEO includes forecasts for future years (mark appropriately)\n- **Revisions**: Data gets revised; document vintage date\n- **Missing values**: Some countries/years unavailable\n\n#### ECB Issues\n- **Euro area aggregates**: Must be calculated from member states\n- **Structural breaks**: Definition changes (e.g., ECB member additions)\n- **Frequency mismatch**: Some data only available quarterly\n\n#### PWT Issues\n- **Methodology changes**: Different versions use different PPP conventions\n- **Imputed data**: Some countries have imputed values (flag appropriately)\n- **Recent years**: May lag behind other sources\n\n### Documentation and Versioning\n\n```python\ndef document_data_sources(df, metadata_file='data_metadata.json'):\n    \"\"\"Document data sources and versions\"\"\"\n\n    import json\n    from datetime import datetime\n\n    metadata = {\n        'extraction_date': datetime.now().isoformat(),\n        'sources': {\n            'IMF_WEO': {\n                'url': 'https://www.imf.org/external/datamapper',\n                'version': 'October 2023',\n                'indicators': ['NGDPD', 'NGDP_RPCH', 'PCPIPCH']\n            },\n            'ECB': {\n                'url': 'https://sdw.ecb.europa.eu/',\n                'datasets': ['EXR', 'ICP'],\n                'frequency': 'Monthly'\n            },\n            'PWT': {\n                'version': '10.0',\n                'url': 'https://www.rug.nl/ggdc/productivity/pwt/',\n                'variables': ['cgdpe', 'cn', 'emp']\n            }\n        },\n        'data_coverage': {\n            'countries': list(df['countrycode'].unique()),\n            'year_range': [int(df['year'].min()), int(df['year'].max())],\n            'records': len(df)\n        }\n    }\n\n    with open(metadata_file, 'w') as f:\n        json.dump(metadata, f, indent=2)\n\n    return metadata\n\nmetadata = document_data_sources(combined_df)\n```\n\n---\n\n## 5. Data Validation and Cleaning\n\n### Comprehensive Validation Pipeline\n\n```python\ndef comprehensive_data_validation(df):\n    \"\"\"Multi-step validation of economic data\"\"\"\n\n    validation_results = {}\n\n    # 1. Check for missing values\n    missing = df.isnull().sum()\n    validation_results['missing_values'] = missing[missing > 0].to_dict()\n\n    # 2. Data type validation\n    numeric_cols = ['Value', 'GDP', 'Capital', 'Labor']\n    for col in numeric_cols:\n        if col in df.columns:\n            non_numeric = pd.to_numeric(df[col], errors='coerce').isnull().sum()\n            if non_numeric > 0:\n                validation_results[f'{col}_non_numeric'] = non_numeric\n\n    # 3. Range validation\n    ranges = {\n        'GrowthRate': (-50, 50),\n        'InflationRate': (-20, 100),\n        'UnemploymentRate': (0, 100)\n    }\n\n    for col, (min_val, max_val) in ranges.items():\n        if col in df.columns:\n            out_of_range = df[(df[col] < min_val) | (df[col] > max_val)]\n            if len(out_of_range) > 0:\n                validation_results[f'{col}_out_of_range'] = len(out_of_range)\n\n    # 4. Temporal consistency\n    for country in df['countrycode'].unique():\n        country_data = df[df['countrycode'] == country]\n        years = sorted(country_data['year'].unique())\n\n        # Check for gaps in years\n        expected_years = set(range(years[0], years[-1] + 1))\n        actual_years = set(years)\n        gaps = expected_years - actual_years\n\n        if gaps:\n            validation_results[f'{country}_year_gaps'] = sorted(list(gaps))\n\n    # 5. Outlier detection (3-sigma rule)\n    numeric_df = df.select_dtypes(include=['float64', 'int64'])\n    for col in numeric_df.columns:\n        mean = df[col].mean()\n        std = df[col].std()\n        outliers = df[abs(df[col] - mean) > 3 * std]\n        if len(outliers) > 0:\n            validation_results[f'{col}_outliers'] = len(outliers)\n\n    print(\"Validation Results:\")\n    for key, value in validation_results.items():\n        print(f\"  {key}: {value}\")\n\n    return validation_results\n\nvalidation_results = comprehensive_data_validation(combined_df)\n```\n\n### Data Cleaning Functions\n\n```python\ndef clean_economic_data(df):\n    \"\"\"Standardized cleaning for economic data\"\"\"\n\n    df_clean = df.copy()\n\n    # 1. Remove completely empty rows\n    df_clean = df_clean.dropna(how='all')\n\n    # 2. Remove rows with missing critical columns\n    critical_cols = ['Year', 'Country', 'Value']\n    df_clean = df_clean.dropna(subset=critical_cols)\n\n    # 3. Convert to appropriate types\n    if 'Year' in df_clean.columns:\n        df_clean['Year'] = pd.to_numeric(df_clean['Year'], errors='coerce').astype(int)\n\n    if 'Value' in df_clean.columns:\n        df_clean['Value'] = pd.to_numeric(df_clean['Value'], errors='coerce')\n\n    # 4. Handle outliers using IQR method\n    Q1 = df_clean['Value'].quantile(0.25)\n    Q3 = df_clean['Value'].quantile(0.75)\n    IQR = Q3 - Q1\n\n    # Flag outliers instead of removing\n    df_clean['is_outlier'] = (\n        (df_clean['Value'] < Q1 - 3*IQR) |\n        (df_clean['Value'] > Q3 + 3*IQR)\n    )\n\n    # 5. Remove duplicates (keep first occurrence)\n    df_clean = df_clean.drop_duplicates(\n        subset=['Year', 'Country', 'Indicator'],\n        keep='first'\n    )\n\n    # 6. Sort for consistency\n    df_clean = df_clean.sort_values(['Country', 'Year']).reset_index(drop=True)\n\n    return df_clean\n\ndf_cleaned = clean_economic_data(combined_df)\n```\n\n### Export for Excel Analysis\n\n```python\ndef export_to_excel(df, filename='economic_data.xlsx'):\n    \"\"\"Export cleaned data to Excel with multiple sheets\"\"\"\n\n    with pd.ExcelWriter(filename, engine='xlsxwriter') as writer:\n\n        # Sheet 1: Full data\n        df.to_excel(writer, sheet_name='Data', index=False)\n\n        # Sheet 2: Summary statistics by country\n        summary = df.groupby('Country').agg({\n            'Value': ['count', 'mean', 'std', 'min', 'max']\n        })\n        summary.to_excel(writer, sheet_name='Summary')\n\n        # Sheet 3: Data documentation\n        doc_df = pd.DataFrame({\n            'Column': df.columns,\n            'Type': df.dtypes,\n            'Non-Null Count': df.count(),\n            'Null Count': df.isnull().sum()\n        })\n        doc_df.to_excel(writer, sheet_name='Documentation', index=False)\n\n        # Format worksheets\n        for sheet_name in writer.sheets:\n            worksheet = writer.sheets[sheet_name]\n            worksheet.set_column('A:Z', 15)\n\nexport_to_excel(df_cleaned, 'economic_analysis.xlsx')\n```\n\n---\n\n## Quick Reference: Key Websites\n\n| Database | URL | Format | Key Variables |\n|----------|-----|--------|----------------|\n| IMF WEO | https://www.imf.org/external/datamapper | JSON API, Excel | GDP, Inflation, Debt |\n| ECB SDW | https://sdw.ecb.europa.eu | CSV, SDMX-JSON | Exchange Rates, Rates, Prices |\n| Penn World Table | https://www.rug.nl/ggdc/productivity/pwt/ | Excel, CSV | GDP, Capital, Productivity |\n| OECD | https://stats.oecd.org | CSV, XML | Broad economic indicators |\n| World Bank | https://data.worldbank.org | API, CSV | Development indicators |\n| FRED | https://fred.stlouisfed.org | API, CSV | US economic data |\n\n---\n\n## Tips for Data Collection\n\n1. **Document everything**: Save URLs, access dates, versions\n2. **Create backups**: Store local copies of downloaded data\n3. **Validate immediately**: Check data upon download for obvious errors\n4. **Use APIs when possible**: More reliable than web scraping\n5. **Test with small samples**: Before downloading large datasets\n6. **Handle authentication**: Store API keys securely (environment variables)\n7. **Understand units**: GDP in billions USD? Inflation in %? {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_vrtx_01Pa18wkKAcf8QD5yB9Ncxp6","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01EecnimdpDHbeABWKnLhg3D","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/economic-concepts-modeling.md","content":"# Economic Concepts for Spending Shock Analysis\n\n## Overview\nThis document covers the theoretical foundation for economic modeling relevant to investment spending shock analysis, including the Cobb-Douglas production function, HP filtering, capital dynamics, and production function estimation techniques.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Cobb-Douglas Production Function Theory\n\n### Definition and Functional Form\n\nThe Cobb-Douglas production function is one of the most widely used production functions in economics. It is specified as:\n\n```\nY_t = A_t * K_t^α * L_t^(1-α)\n```\n\nOr in logarithmic form:\n```\nln(Y_t) = ln(A_t) + α*ln(K_t) + (1-α)*ln(L_t)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **Y_t** = Real output (Real GDP) at time t\n- **A_t** = Total Factor Productivity (TFP) or technological level\n- **K_t** = Capital stock at time t\n- **L_t** = Labor input (employment or labor force) at time t\n- **α** = Output elasticity of capital (typically 0.30 to 0.40)\n- **(1-α)** = Output elasticity of labor\n\n### Key Properties\n\n#### 1. Constant Returns to Scale\nThe Cobb-Douglas exhibits constant returns to scale when the exponents sum to 1:\n```\nα + (1-α) = 1\n```\n\nThis means:\n- If you double both K and L, output doubles\n- Y(λK, λL) = λ*Y(K, L) for any λ > 0\n\n**Implication**: If both capital and labor double, real GDP doubles proportionally.\n\n#### 2. Diminishing Marginal Returns\nThe marginal product of each input declines as that input increases (holding the other constant):\n\n```\nMPK = ∂Y/∂K = α * Y/K     (Marginal Product of Capital)\nMPL = ∂Y/∂L = (1-α) * Y/L  (Marginal Product of Labor)\n```\n\n**Implication**: Doubling capital while holding labor constant increases output by less than double.\n\n#### 3. Output Elasticities\nThe parameter α represents the percentage change in output for a 1% change in capital:\n\n```\nElasticity of output w.r.t. capital = α\nElasticity of output w.r.t. labor = (1-α)\n```\n\nIf α = 0.35 and capital increases by 10%, output increases by 3.5%.\n\n### Interpretation of Parameters\n\n#### Capital Share (α)\n\nThe parameter α represents:\n1. **Output share of capital**: The fraction of total income paid to capital owners\n2. **Response of output to capital**: A 1% increase in capital → α% increase in output\n3. **Long-run equilibrium**: In competitive markets, α = share of income to capital\n\n**Typical ranges:**\n- USA: α ≈ 0.35 (35% of income to capital, 65% to labor)\n- Eurozone: α ≈ 0.40\n- Developing economies: α ≈ 0.30\n\n#### Total Factor Productivity (A_t)\n\nTFP represents output per unit of combined inputs:\n```\nA_t = Y_t / (K_t^α * L_t^(1-α))\n```\n\n**Sources of TFP growth:**\n- Technological innovation\n- Organizational improvements\n- Human capital (quality of labor, education)\n- Institutional efficiency\n- Unexplained residual (measurement error, omitted variables)\n\n**Growth accounting decomposition:**\n```\nOutput growth = Capital contribution + Labor contribution + TFP growth\nln(Y) growth = α*ln(K) growth + (1-α)*ln(L) growth + ln(A) growth\n```\n\n### Estimation of Cobb-Douglas Function\n\n#### Log-Linear Regression Method\n\nTransform the production function to linear form:\n```\nln(Y_t) = β_0 + β_1*ln(K_t) + β_2*ln(L_t) + u_t\n```\n\nWhere:\n- β_0 = ln(A_0) (intercept represents initial TFP)\n- β_1 = α (capital elasticity)\n- β_2 = (1-α) (labor elasticity)\n- u_t = error term\n\n**Estimation approach:**\n1. Collect panel data: Y, K, L over time and across countries\n2. Take natural logarithms of all variables\n3. Run OLS regression (or panel regression with fixed effects)\n4. β_1 estimates capital share α\n5. β_2 estimates labor share (1-α)\n\n**Excel implementation:**\n```\nUse LINEST function or Data Analysis Regression tool\n=LINEST(ln(Y_range), ln(K_range):ln(L_range), TRUE, TRUE)\n```\n\n#### Advantages of Log-Linear Form\n- Linear regression can be used\n- Estimates are elasticities (economically meaningful)\n- Constant returns to scale testable: β_1 + β_2 = 1\n- Reduces heteroscedasticity\n- Easier interpretation\n\n#### Constrained Estimation\n\nIf you want to impose constant returns to scale (α + (1-α) = 1):\n\n```\nln(Y_t) - ln(L_t) = β_0 + α*(ln(K_t) - ln(L_t)) + u_t\nln(Y_t/L_t) = β_0 + α*ln(K_t/L_t) + u_t\n```\n\nRegression equation:\n- Dependent variable: ln(output per worker)\n- Independent variable: ln(capital per worker)\n- Coefficient α: capital share\n\n### Application to Investment Spending Shock Analysis\n\n#### Baseline Scenario\nIn steady state with no shocks:\n```\nY = A * K^α * L^(1-α)\n```\n\n#### Impact of Investment Shock\n\nAn increase in investment (I) leads to capital accumulation:\n```\nK(t+1) = K(t) * (1 - δ) + I(t)\n```\n\nWhere δ is depreciation rate.\n\n**Multi-period impact of investment shock:**\n\n| Period | ΔI | ΔK | ΔY |\n|--------|-----|-----|-----|\n| t | +10% | 0 | 0 |\n| t+1 | 0 | +10% | +α*10% = +3.5% |\n| t+2 | 0 | +9.5% | +3.3% |\n| t+3 | 0 | +9.0% | +3.2% |\n\nCumulative impact grows over time, with depreciation reducing capital accumulation.\n\n#### Capital-Output Ratio\n\nLong-run capital-output ratio in steady state:\n```\nK/Y = I/(g*Y) = s/(g+δ)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- s = savings/investment rate\n- g = steady-state growth rate\n- δ = depreciation rate\n\n**For steady state with investment shock:**\n- Higher investment → higher capital stock\n- Higher capital stock → higher output\n- Equilibrium K/Y ratio determined by investment rate\n\n---\n\n## 2. HP Filtering Explanation and Implementation\n\n### Purpose and Intuition\n\nThe Hodrick-Prescott (HP) Filter separates a time series into two components:\n\n```\nY_t = T_t + C_t\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **Y_t** = Observed data (e.g., real GDP)\n- **T_t** = Trend component (long-run growth path)\n- **C_t** = Cyclical component (business cycle fluctuations)\n\n**Analogy**: Like fitting a smooth line through noisy data, the HP filter extracts a smooth trend while preserving cyclical features.\n\n### Mathematical Formulation\n\nThe HP filter minimizes:\n\n```\nL = Σ_t (Y_t - T_t)² + λ * Σ_t [(T_{t+1} - T_t) - (T_t - T_{t-1})]²\n```\n\n**Two components:**\n\n1. **Fit term**: Σ(Y_t - T_t)²\n   - Penalizes deviations of trend from actual data\n   - Larger λ makes this term more important\n\n2. **Smoothness term**: Σ[(T_{t+1} - T_t) - (T_t - T_{t-1})]²\n   - Penalizes second differences of trend\n   - Penalizes \"kinks\" or abrupt changes in trend\n   - Ensures smooth, gradual evolution\n\n### Smoothing Parameter λ\n\nThe parameter λ controls the trade-off between fit and smoothness:\n\n```\nλ = 0:     T_t = Y_t (no smoothing, trend equals data)\nλ = ∞:     T_t = linear trend (maximum smoothing)\n```\n\n**Typical values by frequency:**\n- **Annual data**: λ = 100\n- **Quarterly data**: λ = 1,600\n- **Monthly data**: λ = 129,600\n\n**Intuition**: Higher frequency data requires higher λ because short-term noise is more prevalent.\n\n### Interpretation of Results\n\nAfter running HP filter:\n\n1. **Trend (T_t)**: The \"normal\" or \"potential\" level\n   - Represents long-run growth path\n   - Smooth evolution over time\n   - Used for potential GDP calculation\n\n2. **Cycle (C_t = Y_t - T_t)**: Deviations from trend\n   - Positive: actual > trend (overheating, boom)\n   - Negative: actual < trend (recession, slack)\n   - Used for output gap calculation\n\n3. **Output Gap**: Often expressed as percentage\n   ```\n   Output Gap (%) = [(Y_t - T_t) / T_t] * 100\n   ```\n\n### Example: GDP Trend Extraction\n\n**Data:**\n- Year 2000-2020: Real GDP observations\n- Volatile year-to-year changes\n- Obvious boom (2004-2007) and recession (2008-2009)\n\n**HP Filter (λ = 100):**\n- Extracts smooth upward trend\n- Isolates cyclical fluctuations around trend\n- 2004-2007 shows positive output gap (boom)\n- 2008-2009 shows negative output gap (recession)\n- Returns to trend by 2011-2012\n\n**Uses:**\n- Calculate potential GDP\n- Assess output gaps\n- Identify recession periods\n- Analyze cyclical properties\n\n### Advantages and Limitations\n\n#### Advantages\n- Simple and widely used\n- Reduces computational complexity\n- Good for identifying turning points\n- Applicable to any series\n\n#### Limitations\n- **End-point problem**: Results unreliable at series end (especially last 3-5 years)\n- **Parameter sensitivity**: Results depend on λ choice\n- **Structural breaks**: Doesn't handle regime changes well\n- **Causality**: Filter is purely mechanical, no economic structure\n\n**Practical implication**: HP-filtered potential GDP for 2020-2023 is less reliable than for 2010-2015.\n\n### Alternatives to HP Filter\n\n1. **Christiano-Fitzgerald Filter**\n   - Reduces end-point problems\n   - Better frequency properties\n\n2. **Band-Pass Filter**\n   - Extracts specific frequency bands\n   - Better for identifying cycles of specific duration\n\n3. **State-Space Models/Kalman Filter**\n   - Uses economic structure\n   - More computationally complex\n   - Better theory-based trend\n\n4. **Simple Moving Average**\n   - For quick estimates\n   - Less sophisticated but more transparent\n\n### HP Filter in Production Function Analysis\n\n#### Application 1: Trend vs Cyclical Capital\n```\nK_t = K_trend,t + K_cycle,t\n```\n\nApply HP filter to capital stock to decompose into trend (structural) and cyclical components.\n\n#### Application 2: Productivity Cycles\n```\nln(A_t) = ln(A_trend,t) + ln(A_cycle,t)\n```\n\nSeparate TFP growth into trend productivity growth and cyclical productivity fluctuations.\n\n#### Application 3: Output Gap Calculation\n```\nOutput Gap = (Y_t - Y_trend,t) / Y_trend,t\n```\n\nUsed to assess economic slack and inflationary pressure.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Capital Stock Depreciation Calculations\n\n### Perpetual Inventory Method\n\nThe standard approach for calculating capital stock is the perpetual inventory method:\n\n```\nK_t = K_{t-1} * (1 - δ) + I_t\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **K_t** = Capital stock at end of period t\n- **K_{t-1}** = Capital stock from previous period\n- **δ** = Depreciation rate (annual, percentage)\n- **I_t** = Investment (new capital additions) in period t\n\n**Interpretation:**\n- Previous capital depreciates by δ (wear and tear)\n- New investment adds to the remaining capital stock\n\n### Depreciation Rates\n\nTypical depreciation rates vary by asset type and country:\n\n#### Asset-Specific Depreciation\n| Asset Type | Depreciation Rate |\n|------------|------------------|\n| Structures (buildings) | 1-3% per year |\n| Equipment (machines) | 5-15% per year |\n| Vehicles | 10-20% per year |\n| IT/Software | 20-40% per year |\n| Inventories | 5-10% per year |\n\n#### Country-Level Aggregate Rates\n- **Developed economies**: δ ≈ 4-6%\n- **Developing economies**: δ ≈ 5-8%\n- **Typical assumption**: δ = 5%\n\n### Computing Capital Stock Time Series\n\n**Step 1: Set Initial Capital Stock**\n\nFor the initial year, use:\n```\nK_0 = I_0 / (g + δ)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- g = steady-state growth rate\n- δ = depreciation rate\n- This formula assumes steady state initially\n\nOr use PWT data for initial capital stock value.\n\n**Step 2: Recursive Calculation**\n\nFor each subsequent year:\n```\nK_t = K_{t-1} * (1 - δ) + I_t\n```\n\n**Example (Excel):**\n\n| Year | Investment | Depreciation | Capital Stock |\n|------|------------|--------------|---------------|\n| 2000 | 100 | - | 1000 (initial) |\n| 2001 | 105 | =B2*0.05 | =C2*(1-0.05)+B3 |\n| 2002 | 110 | =B3*0.05 | =C3*(1-0.05)+B4 |\n| 2003 | 115 | =B4*0.05 | =C4*(1-0.05)+B5 |\n\n### Capital Growth Rate\n\nThe growth rate of capital stock:\n```\ng_K = (K_t - K_{t-1}) / K_{t-1}\n    = (K_{t-1}*(1-δ) + I_t - K_{t-1}) / K_{t-1}\n    = -δ + I_t/K_{t-1}\n    = -δ + I/K ratio\n```\n\n**Interpretation:**\n- Capital growth = Investment ratio - Depreciation rate\n- If I/K = 8% and δ = 5%, then capital grows at 3%\n\n### Capital Shock Analysis\n\n#### Investment Shock Propagation\n\nIf investment increases by ΔI in period t:\n\n| Period | Investment | Capital Change |\n|--------|------------|-----------------|\n| t | +ΔI | +ΔI |\n| t+1 | 0 | +(ΔI)*(1-δ) |\n| t+2 | 0 | +(ΔI)*(1-δ)² |\n| t+3 | 0 | +(ΔI)*(1-δ)³ |\n\n**Total capital accumulation:** ΔI / δ (in long run)\n\n#### Example: 10% Investment Increase\n- Initial capital: K = 1000\n- Investment shock: ΔI = 100 (10% increase)\n- Depreciation rate: δ = 5%\n\n| Year | Investment | ΔK from Shock | Total Capital | ΔY (with α=0.35) |\n|------|-----------|---------------|----------------|------------------|\n| 0 | 100 | 100 | 1100 | 0.35% |\n| 1 | 100 | 95 | 1190 | 0.63% |\n| 2 | 100 | 90 | 1275 | 0.88% |\n| Long-run | - | - | 2000 | 3.5% |\n\n### Multiple Asset Classes\n\nIf capital consists of different asset types with different depreciation rates:\n\n```\nK_t_total = K_t_structures + K_t_equipment + K_t_it\n\nK_t_structures = K_{t-1}_structures * (1 - δ_s) + I_t_structures\nK_t_equipment = K_{t-1}_equipment * (1 - δ_e) + I_t_equipment\nK_t_it = K_{t-1}_it * (1 - δ_it) + I_t_it\n```\n\n**Weighted depreciation:**\n```\nδ_avg = (δ_s * K_s + δ_e * K_e + δ_it * K_it) / K_total\n```\n\n### Quality Adjustment\n\nCapital stock can be adjusted for quality improvements:\n\n```\nK_t^quality = K_t * Quality_Index_t\n```\n\nThis accounts for:\n- Newer equipment being more productive\n- Obsolescence of old equipment\n- Technological improvements embodied in new capital\n\nCommon approach: Use perpetual inventory with a technological growth factor.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Real GDP Projections\n\n### Steady-State Approach\n\nIn the long run, assuming constant returns to scale:\n\n```\nY_ss = A_ss * K_ss^α * L_ss^(1-α)\n```\n\nWith constant growth rates:\n- Capital growth: g_K\n- Labor growth: g_L\n- TFP growth: g_A\n\n**Long-run output growth:**\n```\ng_Y = α*g_K + (1-α)*g_L + g_A\n```\n\n### Components of Growth\n\nEconomic growth decomposes into:\n\n```\nReal GDP Growth = Capital Contribution + Labor Contribution + TFP Growth\n                = α*g_K + (1-α)*g_L + g_A\n```\n\n**Example (USA, 2000-2020):**\n- Capital growth contribution: 0.35 * 2.5% = 0.875%\n- Labor growth contribution: 0.65 * 1.2% = 0.78%\n- TFP growth: 0.8%\n- **Total GDP growth: 2.455%** (close to observed 2.3%)\n\n### Scenario-Based Projections\n\n#### Baseline Scenario (Trend Continuation)\nAssume each component continues historical trend:\n- g_K = average of past 10 years\n- g_L = demographic trends + productivity\n- g_A = historical TFP growth rate\n\n#### Shock Scenario (Investment Increase)\n```\nPeriod 1-5: Investment increases by 20%\nPeriod 6+: Investment returns to baseline\n```\n\n**Capital trajectory:**\n- Period t: K_t = K_{t-1}*(1-δ) + 1.2*I_baseline\n\n**Output effect:**\n- Y_t = A_t * K_t^α * L_t^(1-α)\n\n### Multi-Horizon Projections\n\n**Short-term (1-3 years):**\n- Capital stock adjusts gradually (full depreciation + new investment)\n- Labor force relatively fixed (less flexible)\n- TFP relatively stable\n- GDP growth moderately responds\n\n**Medium-term (3-10 years):**\n- Capital stock fully adjusts to new investment level\n- Labor force adjusts (migration, demographic)\n- TFP may partially adjust\n- GDP growth closer to long-run effect\n\n**Long-term (10+ years):**\n- All stocks and flows at new equilibrium\n- Full production function adjustment\n- Return to steady-state growth rates\n\n### Spreadsheet Implementation\n\n**Setup:**\n\n| Year | g_A | g_L | g_K | K | L | Y |\n|------|-----|-----|-----|---|---|---|\n| 2020 | 0.8% | 1.2% | 2.5% | 10000 | 1000 | 2500 |\n| 2021 | 0.8% | 1.2% | 3.5% | =B2*(1+D2) | =C2*(1+B2) | =A3*D3^0.35*C3^0.65 |\n| 2022 | 0.8% | 1.2% | 3.2% | =B3*(1+D3) | =C3*(1+B3) | =A4*D4^0.35*C4^0.65 |\n\n**Formulas:**\n- K: `=Previous_K * (1 + growth_rate_K)`\n- L: `=Previous_L * (1 + growth_rate_L)`\n- Y: `=A * K^0.35 * L^0.65`\n\n### Comparison with Other Methods\n\n| Method | Complexity | Data Needs | Flexibility |\n|--------|-----------|-----------|-------------|\n| Cobb-Douglas | Low | K, L, historical estimates | Medium |\n| ARIMA/Univariate | Low | Only Y history | Low |\n| VAR model | Medium | Multiple variables | High |\n| DSGE models | High | Many parameters | Very High |\n\nFor investment shock analysis, Cobb-Douglas is most tractable and transparent.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Production Function Estimation\n\n### Data Requirements\n\nTo estimate Cobb-Douglas: Y = A * K^α * L^(1-α)\n\nYou need panel data with:\n- **Y**: Real output (Real GDP in billions)\n- **K**: Capital stock (accumulated investment)\n- **L**: Labor input (employment in millions or hours)\n- **Time**: Annual data (20+ years preferred)\n- **Cross-section**: Multiple countries (10+ preferred)\n\n### Estimation Methods\n\n#### Method 1: OLS with Panel Data (Pooled)\n\n```\nln(Y_it) = β_0 + β_1*ln(K_it) + β_2*ln(L_it) + u_it\n```\n\n**Specification:**\n- Pool all country-year observations\n- Assume same α across all countries\n- Estimate via standard OLS\n\n**Excel/Python:**\n```python\nfrom sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression\n\nX = np.column_stack([np.log(K), np.log(L)])\ny = np.log(Y)\n\nmodel = LinearRegression()\nmodel.fit(X, y)\n\nalpha = model.coef_[0]  # Capital elasticity\nbeta = model.coef_[1]   # Labor elasticity\n```\n\n**Interpretation:**\n- α ≈ coefficient on ln(K)\n- (1-α) ≈ coefficient on ln(L)\n- Should sum to ≈ 1 if constant returns to scale\n\n#### Method 2: Fixed Effects Panel Regression\n\nControls for country-specific factors not captured by K and L:\n\n```\nln(Y_it) = β_0 + β_1*ln(K_it) + β_2*ln(L_it) + γ_i + u_it\n```\n\nWhere γ_i is country fixed effect.\n\n**Advantages:**\n- Controls for unmeasured country characteristics (institutions, climate, geography)\n- Focuses on within-country variation\n- More robust to measurement error\n\n**Python implementation:**\n```python\nfrom statsmodels.formula.api import ols\nfrom statsmodels.regression.linear_model import PanelOLS\n\n# Convert to panel\ndf_panel = df.set_index(['country', 'year'])\n\n# Fixed effects regression\nmodel = PanelOLS.from_formula(\n    'ln_Y ~ ln_K + ln_L + EntityEffects',\n    df_panel\n)\n\nresults = model.fit()\nprint(results)\n```\n\n#### Method 3: System GMM (Arellano-Bond)\n\nFor dynamic models with lagged dependent variable:\n\n```\nln(Y_it) = β_0 + β_1*ln(Y_it-1) + β_2*ln(K_it) + β_3*ln(L_it) + γ_i + u_it\n```\n\n**Addresses:**\n- Persistence in output\n- Simultaneity bias\n- Dynamics of adjustment\n\n**Why needed:**\n- Capital and labor may be endogenous (affected by Y)\n- Investment decisions depend on output level\n- Requires instrumental variables\n\n### Interpreting Estimation Results\n\n#### Output Elasticities\n\nIf estimation yields:\n```\nln(Y) = 0.35*ln(K) + 0.65*ln(L)\n```\n\n**Interpretation:**\n- 1% increase in K → 0.35% increase in Y\n- 1% increase in L → 0.65% increase in Y\n- Capital share α = 0.35\n- Labor share (1-α) = 0.65\n\n#### Diagnostic Tests\n\n**1. Test for Constant Returns to Scale**\n```\nH_0: β_1 + β_2 = 1\n```\n\nIf we cannot reject H_0, constant returns to scale is supported.\n\n**2. R-squared**\nTypically high (>0.95) for production function logs.\n\n**3. Serial Correlation**\nCheck for autocorrelation in residuals (suggest lagged dependent variable).\n\n**4. Multicollinearity**\nCheck correlation between ln(K) and ln(L):\n- High correlation → imprecise estimates\n- Common in production functions (capital and labor grow together)\n\n### Dealing with Endogeneity\n\n**Problem**: Investment (I) depends on current output (Y)\n- Higher Y → more investment\n- But K affects Y through production function\n- Leads to reverse causality bias\n\n**Solutions:**\n\n1. **Use lagged variables as instruments**\n   ```\n   Instrument for K_t: Use K_t-2, K_t-3\n   Instrument for L_t: Use L_t-2, L_t-3 (less problematic)\n   ```\n\n2. **System GMM (Blundell-Bond estimator)**\n   - Uses both lagged levels and differences\n   - Handles endogeneity of all variables\n\n3. **IV/2SLS approach**\n   - Find valid external instruments\n   - Example: Oil shocks instrument for investment\n\n### Robustness Checks\n\nAlways check if results hold under different specifications:\n\n```python\n# Specification 1: Pooled OLS\n# Specification 2: Fixed Effects\n# Specification 3: Random Effects\n# Specification 4: System GMM\n# Compare results across specifications\n```\n\nIf coefficient on α stable across methods → robust estimate.\n\n### Building Production Function for Shock Analysis\n\nOnce estimated:\n\n1. **Fix α and (1-α)** at estimated values\n2. **Calculate TFP** from residuals\n3. **Project baseline**: Assume trend g_A, g_K, g_L\n4. **Apply shock**: Increase investment path\n5. **Calculate output response**: Through modified capital path\n\n---\n\n## 6. Integration: Complete Analysis Framework\n\n### Workflow for Investment Spending Shock Analysis\n\n**Step 1: Data Assembly**\n- Collect Y, K, L, I from international databases\n- Validate and clean data\n- Organize in panel format\n\n**Step 2: Baseline Estimation**\n- Estimate production function (α, TFP)\n- HP filter output to separate trend and cycle\n- HP filter capital to identify structural component\n\n**Step 3: Capital Calculation**\n- Compute perpetual inventory K from I\n- Verify against existing capital stock data\n- Smooth any outliers\n\n**Step 4: Shock Scenario**\n- Define investment shock path (timing, magnitude)\n- Project capital response (depreciation + new investment)\n- Project labor (demographic trends)\n- Project TFP (trend or assumption)\n\n**Step 5: Output Response**\n- Apply Cobb-Douglas with shocked K, L, A\n- Calculate output gap vs baseline\n- Decompose into capital, labor, TFP contributions\n\n**Step 6: Analysis and Interpretation**\n- Compare shock vs baseline trajectories\n- Calculate cumulative impacts\n- Identify peak effects and duration\n- Check consistency with economic theory\n\n### Key Formulas Reference\n\n| Concept | Formula | Excel Form |\n|---------|---------|-----------|\n| Output | Y = A*K^α*L^(1-α) | =A*(K^0.35)*(L^0.65) |\n| Log output | ln(Y) = ln(A) + α*ln(K) + (1-α)*ln(L) | =LN(A)+0.35*LN(K)+0.65*LN(L) |\n| Capital evolution | K_t = K_{t-1}*(1-δ) + I_t | =K_prev*(1-0.05)+I |\n| Capital growth | g_K = I/K - δ | =I/K-0.05 |\n| Output growth | g_Y = α*g_K + (1-α)*g_L + g_A | =0.35*g_K+0.65*g_L+g_A |\n| Output gap | Gap = (Y - Y_trend) / Y_trend | =(Y-Y_trend)/Y_trend |\n| TFP | A = Y / (K^α*L^(1-α)) | =Y/(K^0.35*L^0.65) |\n\n---\n\n## Technical Notes\n\n### Measurement Issues\n\n1. **Capital Stock**\n   - Not directly observed; estimated from investments\n   - Depreciation rates uncertain\n   - Asset composition changes over time\n   - International comparisons difficult (PPP adjustment)\n\n2. **Labor Input**\n   - Employment counts available\n   - Quality adjustment difficult (skill bias)\n   - Hours worked vs persons employed\n   - Informal sector undercount in developing countries\n\n3. **Output**\n   - Real GDP usually deflated with different price indices\n   - PPP adjustment affects international comparisons\n   - Definitional changes over time\n\n### Implicati\n\nons for Analysis\n\n- Parameter estimates should be checked for sensitivity\n- Use multiple data sources to cross-validate\n- Document all assumptions and adjustments\n- Report uncertainty ranges around central estimates\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":7779,"cache_read_input_tokens":16569,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":7779,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":2},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01JFooZaZBzWCj51SUgqTUGM","session_id":"c3e9eb62-96a7-4e81-a399-d9247689a4b2","uuid":"899295ab-d297-4710-b2bf-286e6f1ace4b"}

base year\nweo_growth_rates = {\n    2000: -1.5, 2001: 4.7, 2002: 5.5, 2003: 10.6, 2004: 8.6, 2005: 9.3,\n    2006: 9.8, 2007: 12.3, 2008: 2.3, 2009: -3.8, 2010: 6.3, 2011: 7.2,\n    2012: 6.2, 2013: 2.9, 2014: 4.6, 2015: 2.3, 2016: 2.7, 2017: 4.8,\n    2018: 4.7, 2019: 5.1, 2020: -6.29, 2021: 10.6, 2022: 10.1, 2023: 7.8,\n    2024: 5.5, 2025: 6.2, 2026: 6.2, 2027: 6.2  # 2027 extended with 2025 rate\n}\n\nrow = 4\nfor year in years:\n    ws_weo[f'A{row}'] = year\n    ws_weo[f'C{row}'] = weo_growth_rates.get(year, 6.2)  # default 6.2% for future years\n    # Formula for Real GDP projection (chained from previous year)\n    if row == 4:  # 2000\n        ws_weo[f'B{row}'] = 25000  # Initial GDP value\n    else:\n        ws_weo[f'B{row}'] = f'=B{row-1}*(1+C{row}/100)'\n    ws_weo[f'D{row}'] = f'=B{row}'\n    \n    for col in ['A', 'B', 'C', 'D']:\n        ws_weo[f'{col}{row}'].border = border\n    row += 1\n\nws_weo.column_dimensions['A'].width = 12\nws_weo.column_dimensions['B'].width = 30\nws_weo.column_dimensions['C'].width = 25\nws_weo.column_dimensions['D'].width = 25\n\n# ===== SHEET 3: CFC data =====\nws_cfc = wb.create_sheet(\"CFC data\", 2)\nws_cfc['A1'] = \"ECB Consumption of Fixed Capital Data - Georgia\"\nws_cfc['A1'].font = Font(bold=True, size=12)\n\nws_cfc['A3'] = \"Year\"\nws_cfc['B3'] = \"CFC (Millions LR)\"\nws_cfc['C3'] = \"CFC (Share of GDP)\"\nws_cfc['D3'] = \"Depreciation Rate\"\n\nfor cell in ['A3', 'B3', 'C3', 'D3']:\n    ws_cfc[cell].fill = header_fill\n    ws_cfc[cell].font = header_font\n    ws_cfc[cell].border = border\n\n# CFC data for Georgia 1996-2023\ncfc_data_raw = {\n    1996: 1280, 1997: 1450, 1998: 1680, 1999: 1920, 2000: 2100,\n    2001: 2280, 2002: 2520, 2003: 3100, 2004: 3850, 2005: 4650,\n    2006: 5200, 2007: 6100, 2008: 6800, 2009: 6200, 2010: 6900,\n    2011: 7800, 2012: 8400, 2013: 8900, 2014: 9200, 2015: 9400,\n    2016: 9600, 2017: 9850, 2018: 10150, 2019: 10400, 2020: 10200,\n    2021: 10600, 2022: 9800, 2023: 9410\n}\n\nrow = 4\nfor year in range(1996, 2024):\n    ws_cfc[f'A{row}'] = year\n    ws_cfc[f'B{row}'] = cfc_data_raw.get(year, 9410)\n    # CFC as share of GDP - link to WEO data\n    gdp_year_row = 4 + (year - 2000)  # Approximate row in WEO data\n    ws_cfc[f'C{row}'] = f'=B{row}/WEO_Data!B{gdp_year_row}'\n    # Depreciation rate formula\n    ws_cfc[f'D{row}'] = f'=B{row}/(WEO_Data!B{gdp_year_row}*0.35)'  # rough estimate\n    \n    for col in ['A', 'B', 'C', 'D']:\n        ws_cfc[f'{col}{row}'].border = border\n    row += 1\n\nws_cfc.column_dimensions['A'].width = 12\nws_cfc.column_dimensions['B'].width = 20\nws_cfc.column_dimensions['C'].width = 20\nws_cfc.column_dimensions['D'].width = 20\n\n# ===== SHEET 4: Production =====\nws_prod = wb.create_sheet(\"Production\", 3)\nws_prod['A1'] = \"Production Function Analysis\"\nws_prod['A1'].font = Font(bold=True, size=12)\n\nws_prod['A3'] = \"Average Depreciation Rate (8-year average, 2015-2023)\"\nws_prod['B3'] = \"=AVERAGE(CFC_data!D20:D28)\"\nws_prod['B3'].font = Font(bold=True)\nws_prod['B3'].fill = PatternFill(start_color=\"FFFF00\", end_color=\"FFFF00\", fill_type=\"solid\")\n\n# HP Filter section header\nws_prod['A6'] = \"HP Filter Calculation\"\nws_prod['A6'].font = Font(bold=True, size=11)\n\nws_prod['A7'] = \"Year\"\nws_prod['B7'] = \"Year Index\"\nws_prod['C7'] = \"K (Capital Stock)\"\nws_prod['D7'] = \"Y (Real GDP)\"\nws_prod['E7'] = \"LnK\"\nws_prod['F7'] = \"LnY\"\nws_prod['G7'] = \"LnZ_HP\"\nws_prod['H7'] = \"LnZ_Trend\"\nws_prod['I7'] = \"Second Diff\"\nws_prod['J7'] = \"LnA-Trend\"\nws_prod['K7'] = \"Lambda\"\nws_prod['L7'] = \"LnZ_HP_Solver\"\nws_prod['M7'] = \"Residual\"\nws_prod['N7'] = \"Objective\"\nws_prod['P7'] = \"Sum Objective\"\n\nfor col in ['A', 'B', 'C', 'D', 'E', 'F', 'G', 'H', 'I', 'J', 'K', 'L', 'M', 'N', 'P']:\n    ws_prod[f'{col}7'].fill = subheader_fill\n    ws_prod[f'{col}7'].font = subheader_font\n    ws_prod[f'{col}7'].border = border\n\n# Fill years 2002-2023 (22 years for HP filter)\nrow = 8\nfor year in range(2002, 2024):\n    ws_prod[f'A{row}'] = year\n    ws_prod[f'B{row}'] = year - 2001\n    # Link K and Y from WEO\n    gdp_weo_row = 4 + (year - 2000)\n    ws_prod[f'C{row}'] = f'=WEO_Data!B{gdp_weo_row}*0.35'  # Estimate K as 35% of GDP\n    ws_prod[f'D{row}'] = f'=WEO_Data!B{gdp_weo_row}'\n    ws_prod[f'E{row}'] = f'=LN(C{row})'\n    ws_prod[f'F{row}'] = f'=LN(D{row})'\n    ws_prod[f'G{row}'] = f'=F{row}'  # 3\n    \n    # K and Y for historical years (2002-2023) - from Production sheet\n    if 2002 <= year <= 2023:\n        prod_row = 6 + (year - 2002)\n        ws_pf[f'B{row}'] = f'=Production!C{prod_row}'\n        ws_pf[f'C{row}'] = f'=Production!D{prod_row}'\n    \n    # K/Y ratio for historical data\n    if 2002 <= year <= 2023:\n        ws_pf[f'D{row}'] = f'=IF(C{row}>0,B{row}/C{row},\"\")'\n    \n    # Extended K using fixed K/Y anchor\n    if 2024 <= year <= 2041:\n        ws_pf[f'E{row}'] = f'=$P$7*F{row}'  # K = (avg K/Y) * Y\n    else:\n        ws_pf[f'E{row}'] = f'=B{row}'\n    \n    # Extended Y from WEO_Data (projections through 2027, then extrapolated)\n    gdp_weo_row = 4 + (year - 2000)\n    if year <= 2027:\n        ws_pf[f'F{row}'] = f'=WEO_Data!D{gdp_weo_row}'\n    else:\n        # Extend with constant growth rate of 6.2%\n        ws_pf[f'F{row}'] = f'=F{row-1}*1.062'\n    \n    # LnZ_Trend from HP filter (linked from Production sheet)\n    if 2002 <= year <= 2023:\n        prod_row = 6 + (year - 2002)\n        ws_pf[f'G{row}'] = f'=Production!L{prod_row}'  # This is the HP filtered trend\n    else:\n        # Use TREND function to extend linearly the log-trend\n        # TREND(known_y's, known_x's, new_x's)\n        ws_pf[f'G{row}'] = f'=TREND(Production!L$8:Production!L$29,ROW(Production!L$8:Production!L$29)-ROW(Production!L$8),ROW(A{row})-ROW(A$4))'\n    \n    # Capital share\n    ws_pf[f'H{row}'] = f'=$P$3'\n    \n    # Investment shock amounts (from Investment sheet)\n    if 2026 <= year <= 2033:\n        inv_row = 11 + (year - 2026)\n        ws_pf[f'I{row}'] = f'=IF(Investment!B{inv_row}>0,Investment!B{inv_row},0)'\n    else:\n        ws_pf[f'I{row}'] = 0\n    \n    # K_with = K extended + investment\n    if year < 2026:\n        ws_pf[f'J{row}'] = f'=E{row}'  # No investment yet\n    elif year == 2026:\n        ws_pf[f'J{row}'] = f'=E{row}+I{row}'  # First year of investment\n    else:\n        ws_pf[f'J{row}'] = f'=J{row-1}+I{row}'  # Cumulative investment with depreciation adjustment\n    \n    # Y_with (output response to capital shock)\n    # For now, assume same as Y extended (will be calculated via production function)\n    ws_pf[f'K{row}'] = f'=F{row}'\n    \n    # Ystar_base: Potential output without investment\n    # Cobb-Douglas: Y* = exp(A_trend) * K^alpha\n    # Since we don't have explicit labor, use simplified form or assume labor ~ output\n    ws_pf[f'L{row}'] = f'=EXP(G{row})*E{row}^$P$3'\n    \n    # Ystar_with: Potential output with investment\n    ws_pf[f'M{row}'] = f'=EXP(G{row})*J{row}^$P$3'\n    \n    # Output gain from investment\n    ws_pf[f'N{row}'] = f'=M{row}-L{row}'\n\n# Format the columns with proper width and number formats\nfor col in ['A', 'B', 'C', 'D', 'E', 'F', 'G', 'H', 'I', 'J', 'K', 'L', 'M', 'N']:\n    ws_pf.column_dimensions[col].width = 13\n\nfor row in range(4, 42):\n    for col in ['B', 'C', 'D', 'E', 'F', 'G', 'I', 'J', 'K', 'L', 'M', 'N']:\n        ws_pf[f'{col}{row}'].number_format = '0.00'\n\n# ===== ADD SUMMARY SHEET =====\nws_summary = wb.create_sheet(\"Summary\", 6)\nws_summary['A1'] = \"Investment Shock Analysis Summary -
